Exploring Non-Regular Extensions of Propositional Dynamic Logic with Description-Logics Features
Bartosz Bednarczyk ORCID:0000-0002-8267-7554

Abstract
. We investigate the impact of non-regular path expressions on the decidability of satisfiability checking and querying in description logics extending 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 \mathcal{ALC} . Our primary objects of interest are 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 reg \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}} and 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 vpl \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}} , the extensions of 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 \mathcal{ALC} with path expressions employing, respectively, regular and visibly-pushdown languages. The first one, 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 reg \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}} , is a notational variant of the well-known Propositional Dynamic Logic of Fischer and Ladner. The second one, 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 vpl \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}} , was introduced and investigated by Löding and Serre in 2007. The logic 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 vpl \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}} generalises many known decidable non-regular extensions of 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 reg \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}} . We provide a series of undecidability results. First, we show that decidability of the concept satisfiability problem for 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 vpl \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}} is lost upon adding the seemingly innocent 𝖲𝖾𝗅𝖿 \mathsf{Self} operator. Second, we establish undecidability for the concept satisfiability problem for 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 vpl \mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}} extended with nominals. Interestingly, our undecidability proof relies only on one single non-regular (visibly-pushdown) language, namely on r # ​ s # ​ \coloneqq ​ { r n ​ s n ∣ n ∈ ℕ } \mathit{r}^{\#}\mathit{s}^{\#}\coloneqq\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\} for fixed role names r \mathit{r} and s \mathit{s} . Finally, in contrast to the classical database setting, we establish undecidability of query entailment for queries involving non-regular atoms from r # ​ s # \mathit{r}^{\#}\mathit{s}^{\#} , already in the case of 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 \mathcal{ALC} -TBoxes.



Jul. 20, 2023May 14, 2024

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 20, Issue 2, Paper 7

1.
Introduction

Formal ontologies play a crucial role in artificial intelligence, serving as the backbone of various applications such as the Semantic Web, ontology-based information integration, and peer-to-peer data management.
In reasoning about graph-structured data, a significant role is played by
description logics
(DLs)
[
BHLS17
]
, a robust family of logical formalisms serving as the logical foundation of contemporary standardised ontology languages, including OWL 2 by the W3C
[
GHM+08
,
HKP+12
]
.
Among many features present in extensions of the basic description logic
\mathcal{ALC}
, an especially useful one is
\cdot_{\mathsf{reg}}
, supported by the popular
\mathcal{Z}
-family of description logics
[
CEO09
]
.
With
\cdot_{\mathsf{reg}}
one can specify regular path constraints, allowing the user to navigate graph-structured data.
In recent years, many extensions of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
for ontology-engineering were proposed
[
BCOS14
,
COS16
,
Ort10
]
, and the complexity landscape of their reasoning problems is now mostly well-understood
[
CEO09
,
BR19
,
BK22
]
.
In fact, the logic
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
was already studied in 1979 by the formal-verification community
[
FL79
]
, under the name of Propositional Dynamic Logic (PDL).
Relationship between (extensions of) PDL and
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
were investigated by De Giacomo and Lenzerini
[
DL94
]
.

The spectrum of recognizable word languages is relatively wide. Hence the question of whether regular constraints in path expressions of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
can be lifted to more expressive classes of languages received a lot of attention from researchers.
We call such extensions of PDL
non-regular
.
After the first undecidability proof of satisfiability of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
by context-free languages
[
HPS81b
, Cor. 2.2]
, several decidable cases were identified. For instance, Koren and Pnueli
[
KP83
, Sec. Decidability]
proved that
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
extended with the simplest non-regular language
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\coloneqq\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
for
fixed
roles
\mathit{r},\mathit{s}
is decidable; while combining it with
{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{r}}^{\#}
leads to undecidability
[
HPS81a
, Thm. 3.2]
.
This surprises at first glance, but as it was shown later
[
LLS07
, Thm. 18]
, PDL extended with a broad class of input-driven context-free languages, called
visibly pushdown languages

[
AM09
, Sec. 5]
, remains decidable.
This generalises all previously known decidability results, and partially explains the reason behind known failures (
e
.
g
. the languages
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}
and
{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{r}}^{\#}
cannot be both visibly-pushdown under the same partition of the alphabet).
Three years ago, the decidability boundary was pushed even further
[
BL21
, Ex. 1]
, by allowing for mixing modalities in visibly-pushdown expressions (for instance, allowing the user to specify that “for all positive integers
n\in\mathbb{N}
, all
\mathit{t}
-successors of
\mathit{r}^{n}
-reachable elements can
\mathit{s}^{n}
-reach an element fulfilling
\varphi
”).

Our motivation and our contribution.

Despite the presence of a plethora of various results concerning non-regular extensions of PDL
[
KP83
,
HP84
,
HS96
,
HR93
,
BL21
]
, to the best of our knowledge the extensions of non-regular PDL with popular features supported by W3C ontology languages are yet to be investigated.
Such extensions include, among others,
nominals
(constants),
inverse roles
(inverse programs),
functionality
or
counting
(deterministic programs or graded modalities), and the
\mathsf{Self}
operator (self-loops).
The honourable exception is the unpublished undecidability result for
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
extended with the language
\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}(\mathit{r}^{-})^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, where
\mathit{r}^{-}
denotes the converse of
\mathit{r}
, from Göller’s thesis
[
Göl08
]
(answering an open problem of Demri
[
Dem07
, Probléme ouvert 29]
).
The lack of results on entailment of non-regular queries over ontologies is also intriguing, taking into account positive results for conjunctive visibly-pushdown queries in the setting of relational-databases
[
LL15
, Thm. 2]
.

In this paper we contribute to the further understanding of the aforementioned questions.
Our results are negative.
Section
3
establishes undecidability of the concept satisfiability of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
extended with the seemingly innocent
\mathsf{Self}
operator.
Section
4
establishes undecidability of the concept satisfiability of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
extended with nominals.
More specifically, the undecidability arises already if the only non-regular language present in concepts is
\mathit{r}^{\#}\mathit{s}^{\#}
.
Finally, Section
5
establishes undecidability of the query entailment problem over
\mathcal{ALC}
-TBoxes, in which our queries can employ atoms involving the language
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}
.
On the positive side, a combination of existing works shows that the entailment problem of
\mathbb{REG}
-PEQs over
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
-TBoxes is
2ExpTime
-complete.
We conclude with an open problem list.

This work is a revised and extended version of our JELIA 2023 paper
[
Bed23
]
. In addition to the inclusion of full proofs and minor corrections, the paper contains improved and new undecidability results concerning entailment of non-regular queries (Section
5
).

2.
Preliminaries

We assume familiarity with basics on the description logic
\mathcal{ALC}

[
BHLS17
, Sec. 2.1–2.3]
, regular and context-free languages, Turing machines and computability
[
Sip13
, Sec. 1–5]
.
We employ
\mathbb{N}
to denote the set of
non-negative
integers and
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
to denote the set
\{0,1,\ldots,n{-}1\}
.

Basics on
\mathcal{ALC}
.

We recall ingredients of the well-known description logic
\mathcal{ALC}

[
BHLS17
, Sec. 2.1–2.3]
.
1
1

1

To guide people with modal logic background: concept names are propositional letters, interpretations are Kripke structures, ABoxes (a.k.a. Assertion Boxes) are partial descriptions of Kripke structures, and TBoxes (a.k.a. Terminological Boxes) are limited forms of the universal modality. Consult Section 2.6.2 of the DL Book
[
BHLS17
]
for a more detailed comparison.

We fix countably infinite pairwise disjoint sets of
individual names

\mathbf{N_{I}}
,
concept names

\mathbf{N_{C}}
, and
role names

\mathbf{N_{R}}
and introduce the description logic
\mathcal{ALC}
.
Starting from
\mathbf{N_{C}}
and
\mathbf{N_{R}}
, the set
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathcal{ALC}
-
concepts
is built using the following concept constructors:
negation

(\neg\mathrm{C})
,
conjunction

(\mathrm{C}\sqcap\mathrm{D})
,
existential restriction
(
\exists{\mathit{r}}.\mathrm{C}
), and the
top concept

\top
with the grammar:

\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}\;\Coloneqq\top\;\mid\;\mathrm{A}\;\mid\;\neg\mathrm{C}\;\mid\;\mathrm{C}\sqcap\mathrm{D}\;\mid\;\exists{\mathit{r}}.\mathrm{C},

where
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}\in\mathbf{C}
,
\mathrm{A}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}
and
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
.
We extend the syntax by notational shortcuts for disjunction
\mathrm{C}\sqcup\mathrm{D}\coloneqq\neg(\neg\mathrm{C}\sqcap\neg\mathrm{D})
, universal restrictions
\forall{\mathit{r}}.\mathrm{C}\coloneqq\neg\exists{\mathit{r}}.\neg\mathrm{C}
, the bottom concept
\bot\coloneqq\neg\top
, implication
\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{D}\coloneqq\neg\mathrm{C}\sqcup\mathrm{D}
, and equivalence
\mathrm{C}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{D}\coloneqq(\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{D})\sqcap(\mathrm{D}\to\mathrm{C})
.

Assertions
are (possibly negated) expressions of the form
\mathrm{C}(\texttt{a})
or
\mathit{r}(\texttt{a},\texttt{b})
for
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}\in\mathbf{N_{I}}
,
\mathrm{C}\in\mathbf{C}
and
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
.
A
general concept inclusion
(GCI) has the form
\mathrm{C}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{D}
for concepts
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}\in\mathbf{C}
.
A
knowledge base
(KB)
\mathcal{K}:=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{T})
is composed of a finite set
\mathcal{A}
(
ABox
) of assertions and a finite set
\mathcal{T}
(
TBox
) of GCIs.
We call the elements of
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}

axioms
.

The semantics of
\mathcal{ALC}
is defined via
interpretations

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
composed of a non-empty set
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, called the
domain of
\mathcal{I}
and an
interpretation function

\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
, mapping individual names to elements of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, concept names to subsets of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, and role names to subsets of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
This mapping is then extended to concepts (see Table
1
), and finally used to define
satisfaction
of assertions and GCIs.
More precisely
\mathcal{I}\models\mathrm{C}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{D}
if
\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathrm{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\mathcal{I}\models\mathrm{C}(\texttt{a})
if
\texttt{a}^{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\mathcal{I}\models\mathit{r}(\texttt{a},\texttt{b})
if
(\texttt{a}^{\mathcal{I}},\texttt{b}^{\mathcal{I}})\in\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We say that an interpretation
\mathcal{I}

satisfies
a concept
\mathrm{C}
(or
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of
\mathrm{C}
, written:
\mathcal{I}\models\mathrm{C}
) if
\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
.
A concept is
consistent
(or
satisfiable
) if it has a model, and
inconsistent
(or
unsatisfiable
) otherwise.
In the
consistency
(or
satisfiability
)
problem
we ask, whether an input concept has a model.
Similarly, we say that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies a KB
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{T})
(written:
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{K}
) if
\mathcal{I}
satisfies all axioms of
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
.
The knowledge base satisfiability problem is defined analogously.

Table 1.
Concepts and roles in
\mathcal{ALC}
.

Name

Syntax

Semantics

top concept

\top

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

concept name

\mathrm{A}

\mathrm{A}^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

role name

\mathit{r}

\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}{\times}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

concept negation

\neg\mathrm{C}

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}

concept intersection

\mathrm{C}\sqcap\mathrm{D}

\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathrm{D}^{\mathcal{I}}

existential restriction

\exists{\mathit{r}}.\mathrm{C}

\{\mathrm{d}\mid\exists{\mathrm{e}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}}\ (\mathrm{d},\mathrm{e})\in\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}\}

A path in an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a finite word in
(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}})^{+}
. Given a path
\rho
we enumerate its components with
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}
.
We use
\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert
to denote the length of
\rho
(note that
\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert=|\rho|{-}1
).
We say that
\rho

starts from
(resp.
ends in
)
\mathrm{d}
if
\rho_{1}=\mathrm{d}
holds (resp.
\rho_{|\rho|}=\mathrm{d}
).
If
\mathsf{N}\subseteq\mathbf{N_{I}}
is a set of individual names, we call an element
\mathrm{d}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\mathsf{N}
-named
if
\mathrm{d}=\texttt{a}^{\mathcal{I}}
holds for some
\texttt{a}\in\mathsf{N}
.

\mathcal{ALC}
with extras.

In the next sections we consider several popular description-logics features:
nominals

(\mathcal{O})
, and the
\mathsf{Self}
operator

(\cdot^{\mathsf{Self}})
. Their semantics is recalled in Table
2
, in which
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\texttt{a}\in\mathbf{N_{I}}
, and
\mathrm{C}
is a concept.

Table 2.
Popular description-logics features.

Name

Syntax

Semantics

nominal

\{\texttt{a}\}

\{\texttt{a}^{\mathcal{I}}\}

self-operator

\exists{\mathit{r}}.\mathsf{Self}

\{\mathrm{d}\mid(\mathrm{d},\mathrm{d})\in\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}\}

\mathcal{ALC}
with path expressions.

We treat the set
\Sigma_{\mathsf{all}}\coloneqq\mathbf{N_{R}}\cup\{\mathrm{C}?\mid\mathrm{C}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}\}
as an infinite alphabet.
Let
\mathbb{ALL}
and
\mathbb{REG}
be classes of all Turing-recognizable (resp. all regular) languages of finite words over any finite subset of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{all}}
.
Given a language
\mathdutchbcal{L}\in\mathbb{ALL}
and a path
\rho\coloneqq\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\ldots\rho_{n}\rho_{n{+}1}
in an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
, we say that
\rho
is an
\mathdutchbcal{L}
-
path
, if there exists a word
\mathtt{w}\coloneqq\mathtt{w}_{1}\mathtt{w}_{2}\ldots\mathtt{w}_{n}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}
such that for all indices
i\leq n
we have either (i)
\mathtt{w}_{i}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
and
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i{+}1})\in(\mathtt{w}_{i})^{\mathcal{I}}
, or (ii)
\mathtt{w}_{i}
is of the form
\mathrm{C}?
for some
\mathrm{C}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}
,
\rho_{i}=\rho_{i{+}1}
and
\rho_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Intuitively the word
\mathtt{w}
either traverses roles between the elements of
\rho
or loops at an element to check the satisfaction of concepts.
For convenience, we will also say that
\mathrm{e}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
is
\mathdutchbcal{L}
-
reachable from

\mathrm{d}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
(or alternatively that
\mathrm{d}

\mathdutchbcal{L}
-
reaches

\mathrm{e}
) if there is an
\mathdutchbcal{L}
-path
\rho
that starts from
\mathrm{d}
and ends in
\mathrm{e}
.
The logic
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{all}}
extends
\mathcal{ALC}
with concept constructors of the form
\exists{\mathdutchbcal{L}}.\mathrm{C}
, where
\mathdutchbcal{L}\in\mathbb{ALL}
and
\mathrm{C}
is an
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{all}}
-concept.
Their semantics is as follows:
(\exists{\mathdutchbcal{L}}.\mathrm{C})^{\mathcal{I}}
is the set of all
\mathrm{d}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
that can
\mathdutchbcal{L}
-reach some
\mathrm{e}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}}.\mathrm{C}
stands for
\neg\exists{\mathdutchbcal{L}}.\neg\mathrm{C}
.
The logic
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
is a restriction of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{all}}
to regular languages. It is a notational variant of Propositional Dynamic Logic
[
FL79
]
, popular in the community of formal verification.

Visibly-pushdown languages.

The class
\mathbb{VPL}
of
Visibly-pushdown languages

[
AM09
, Sec. 5]
(VPLs)
is a well-behaved family of context-free languages,
in which the usage of the stack in the underlying automaton model
is input-driven.
For the exposition of VPLs we follow the work of Löding et al.
[
LLS07
, Sec. 2.2]
.
A
pushdown alphabet

\Sigma
is an alphabet equipped with
a partition
(\Sigma_{c},\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{r})
.
The elements of
\Sigma_{c},\Sigma_{i},
and
\Sigma_{r}
are called, respectively,

call
letters,
internal
letters, and
return
letters.
A
visibly-pushdown automaton
(VPA)
\mathdutchbcal{A}
over a pushdown alphabet
\Sigma

is a tuple

(\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{I},\mathtt{F},\Gamma,\mathtt{T})
,
where
\mathtt{Q}
is a finite set of states,

\mathtt{I}
is a finite subset of
initial states
,

\mathtt{F}
is a finite subset of
final states
,

\Gamma
is a finite stack alphabet that contains a

bottom-of-stack
symbol
\lhd
, and

\mathtt{T}
is a transition relation of type

\mathtt{T}\subseteq\left(\mathtt{Q}\times\Sigma_{c}\times\mathtt{Q}\times(\Gamma\setminus\lhd)\right)\;\;\;\cup\;\;\;\left(\mathtt{Q}\times\Sigma_{r}\times\Gamma\times\mathtt{Q})\right)\;\;\;\cup\;\;\;\left(\mathtt{Q}\times\Sigma_{i}\times\mathtt{Q}\right).

A
configuration
of a VPA
\mathdutchbcal{A}
is
a pair
(\mathtt{q},\sigma)\in\mathtt{Q}\times(\Gamma\setminus\lhd)^{*}\lhd

of a state
\mathtt{q}
and a stack content
\sigma
.
Given a letter
\mathtt{a}
and a configuration
(\mathtt{q},\sigma)
we say that
(\mathtt{q}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is an
\mathtt{a}
-successor of
(\mathtt{q},\sigma)
if one of the following cases hold:

•

\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma_{c}
,
\sigma^{\prime}=\gamma\sigma
and there is a transition

(\mathtt{q},\mathtt{a},\mathtt{q}^{\prime},\gamma)\in\mathtt{T}
.

•

\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma_{i}
,
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
and there is a transition

(\mathtt{q},\mathtt{a},\mathtt{q}^{\prime})\in\mathtt{T}
.

•

\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma_{r}
, either
(i)
\sigma=\gamma\sigma^{\prime}
and there is a transition
(\mathtt{q},\mathtt{a},\gamma,\mathtt{q}^{\prime})\in\mathtt{T}
, or (ii)
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=\lhd
and
(\mathtt{q},\mathtt{a},\lhd,\mathtt{q}^{\prime})\in\mathtt{T}
.

We denote this fact by
(\mathtt{q},\sigma)\to_{\mathtt{a}}(\mathtt{q}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
.
Given a finite word
\mathtt{w}\coloneqq\mathtt{a}_{1}\ldots\mathtt{a}_{n}
, a
run
of
\mathdutchbcal{A}
on
\mathtt{w}
is a sequence

(\mathtt{q}_{0},\lhd)\to_{\mathtt{a}_{1}}(\mathtt{q}_{1},\sigma_{1})\to_{\mathtt{a}_{2}}\ldots\to_{\mathtt{a}_{n}}(\mathtt{q}_{n},\sigma_{n})
where
\mathtt{q}_{0}\in\mathtt{I}
.
We call
\mathtt{w}

accepted
by
\mathdutchbcal{A}
if there is a run in which the last configuration contains a final state.
The language
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A})
of
\mathdutchbcal{A}
is composed of all words accepted by
\mathdutchbcal{A}
.
A language
\mathdutchbcal{L}
(
i
.
e
. a set of words) over
\Sigma
is
visibly-pushdown
if there is a VPA
\mathdutchbcal{A}
over
\Sigma
for which
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A})=\mathdutchbcal{L}
.

{exa}

Suppose that
\mathit{r}
is a call letter and
\mathit{s}
is a return letter.
Then the languages
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\coloneqq\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}

and
\mathit{r}^{\#}\mathit{s}^{>\#}\coloneqq\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}^{n{+}m{+}1}\mid n,m\in\mathbb{N}\}

are visibly-pushdown.
Under such a choice of
\mathit{r}
and
\mathit{s}
, the language
{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{r}}^{\#}

is not
visibly-pushdown.
Moreover, every regular language is visibly-pushdown.

The logic
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
.

Throughout the paper,
\Sigma_{\mathsf{all}}
is presented as a pushdown alphabet

\Sigma_{\mathsf{vpl}}\coloneqq\left((\mathbf{N_{R}})_{c},(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{i}\cup\{\mathrm{C}?\mid\mathrm{C}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}\},(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{r}\right),

where the sets
(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{c},(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{i},(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{r}
form a partition of
\mathbf{N_{R}}
.
Hence, we define
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
as the restriction of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{all}}
to visibly-pushdown languages over finite subsets of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{vpl}}
, in which languages in existential and universal restrictions are represented by means of nondeterministic VPA.
The logic
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
generalises many other logics with non-regular path expressions, and has a
2ExpTime
-complete
[
LLS07
, Thm. 18–19]
concept satisfiability problem.
As a special case of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
, we also consider a, rather minimalistic, extension of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
with the language
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}
for one
fixed
call letter
\mathit{r}\in(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{c}
and one
fixed
return letter
\mathit{s}\in(\mathbf{N_{R}})_{r}
.
We denote it here by
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
.
The concept satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
was shown to be decidable
[
KP83
, Sec. Decidability]
already 40 years ago by Koren and Pnueli, but its extensions with popular features like nominals or functionality are still unexplored.

The query entailment problem.

Given a class
\mathbb{C}
of languages, the class of
\mathbb{C}
-enriched Positive Existential Queries
(abbreviated as
\mathbb{C}
-PEQs) is defined with the following syntax:

\mathit{q},\mathit{q}^{\prime}\Coloneqq\bot\;\mid\;\mathrm{A}(\mathit{x})\;\mid\;\mathit{r}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})\;\mid\;\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})\;\mid\;\mathit{q}\vee\mathit{q}^{\prime}\;\mid\;\mathit{q}\land\mathit{q}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{A}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}
,
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
,
\mathdutchbcal{L}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}
are variables from a countably-infinite set
\mathbf{N_{V}}
.
Their semantics is defined as expected,
e
.
g
.
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
evaluates to true under a variable assignment
\eta\colon\mathbf{N_{V}}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
\eta(\mathit{x})
can
\mathdutchbcal{L}
-reach
\eta(\mathit{y})
in
\mathcal{I}
.

\emptyset
-PEQs (or Positive Existential Queries) are a well-known generalization of unions of
conjunctive queries
(in which disjunction is allowed only at the outermost level).

\mathbb{REG}
-PEQs (or Positive Conjunctive Regular Path Queries) are among the most popular query languages nowadays
[
FLS98
,
OS12
]
.
Finally,
\mathbb{VPL}
-PEQs recently received some attention in
[
LL15
]
.
A
\mathbb{C}
-CQ is a disjunction-free
\mathbb{C}
-PEQ.
We say that an interpretation
\mathcal{I}

satisfies
a query
\mathit{q}
(written
\mathcal{I}\models\mathit{q}
), if there exists an assignment
\eta
of variables from
\mathit{q}
to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
under which
\mathit{q}
evaluates to true (a
match
).
A concept
\mathrm{C}
entails a query
\mathit{q}
(written
\mathrm{C}\models\mathit{q}
) if every model of
\mathrm{C}
satisfies
\mathit{q}
.
In total analogy we define the notion of entailment and overload the “
\models
” symbol for the case of TBoxes and knowledge-bases in place of concepts.
For a given description logic
\mathcal{DL}
, the
\mathbb{C}
-PEQ
entailment problem over
\mathcal{DL}
-knowledge-bases
asks to decide if an input
\mathcal{DL}
-knowledge-base entails an input
\mathbb{C}
-PEQ.
The problem’s definition for concepts and TBoxes is analogous.

Tree models.

Löding et al. established that
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
possesses a tree-model property.
An interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is
tree-like
if its domain is a prefix-closed subset of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and for all
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
and
\mathrm{d},\mathrm{e}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
the condition ”if
(\mathrm{d},\mathrm{e})\in\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
\mathrm{e}=\mathrm{d}n
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
” holds.
An interpretation is
single-role
if any two domain elements are connected by at most one role.
The following lemma follows immediately from the proof of Proposition 8 by Löding et al.
[
LLS07
]
.

Corollary 1

(Consequence of the proof of Prop. 8 of
[
LLS07
]
)
.

Every satisfiable
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
-TBox
\mathcal{T}
has a single-role tree-like model.
2
2

2

The original work considers concepts only. However, all their results transfer immediately to the case of TBoxes, as TBoxes can be internalised in concepts in the presence of regular expressions
[
BCM+03
, p. 186]
. The queries are not mentioned either: the so-called tree model property is established with a suitable notion of unravelling, which produces interpretations that can be then homomorphically mapped to the original interpretations (entailing the preservation of the non-satisfaction of query).

Moreover, for any query
\mathit{q}
preserved under homomorphisms we have that
\mathcal{T}\not\models\mathit{q}
if and only if there exists a single-role tree-like model
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models\mathit{q}
.

Undecidability results for extensions of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
that follow from the literature.

Other popular (not yet mentioned) features supported by W3C ontology languages are
inverse roles
and
role hierarchies
.
For bibliographical purposes, we would like to use the extra space given here to briefly discuss how these features result in the undecidability of the respective extensions of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
.
To define the first feature, we associate with each role name
\mathit{r}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
a role name
\mathit{r}^{-}
that is enforced to be interpreted as the inverse of the interpretation of
\mathit{r}
.
It was shown by Stefan Göller in his PhD thesis
[
Göl08
, Prop. 2.32]
that
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
extended with the single visibly-pushdown language
\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}(\mathit{r}^{-})^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is undecidable.

Corollary 2
.

The concept satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
with inverses is undecidable, even if the only allowed non-regular language is
\{\mathit{r}^{n}\mathit{s}(\mathit{r}^{-})^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
for fixed
\mathit{r},\mathit{s}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}
.

The second feature allows for specifying containment of roles by means of expressions of the form for
\mathit{r}\subseteq\mathit{s}
.
The semantics is that
\mathcal{I}\models\mathit{r}\subseteq\mathit{s}
if and only if
\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathit{s}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Fix
\mathit{r}
and
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
to be call letters, and
\mathit{s}
and
\mathit{s}^{\prime}
to be return letters.
Suppose that an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
satisfies all of the statements
\mathit{s}\subseteq\mathit{r}^{\prime}
,
\mathit{r}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{s}
,
\mathit{s}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{r}
, and
\mathit{r}\subseteq\mathit{s}^{\prime}
.
Clearly, for all elements
\mathrm{d}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and concepts
\mathrm{C}
we have that
\mathrm{d}\in(\exists{{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{r}}^{\#}}.\mathrm{C})^{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
\mathrm{d}\in(\exists{{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}^{\prime}}^{\#}}.\mathrm{C})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Thus
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
with role-hierarchies can express concepts of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}
extended with both
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}
and
{\mathit{s}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{r}}^{\#}
.
By undecidability of the concept satisfiability
[
KP83
, Sec. Decidability]
of the latter we conclude:

Corollary 3
.

The concept satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
with role-hierarchies is undecidable, even if the only allowed non-regular languages are
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}
and
{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}^{\prime}}^{\#}
for fixed call letters
\mathit{r},\mathit{r}^{\prime}
and return letters
\mathit{s},\mathit{s}^{\prime}
.

3.
Negative results I: The Seemingly innocent
\mathsf{Self}
operator

We start our series of negative results, by showing (in our opinion) a rather surprising undecidability result.
Henceforth we employ the
\mathsf{Self}
operator, a modelling feature supported by two profiles of the OWL 2 Web Ontology Language
[
HKS06
,
KRH08
]
.
Recall that the
\mathsf{Self}
operator allows us to specify the situation when an element is related to it
self
by a binary relationship,
i
.
e
. we interpret the concept
\exists{\mathit{r}}.\mathsf{Self}
in an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
as the set of all those elements
\mathrm{d}
for which
(\mathrm{d},\mathrm{d})
belongs to
\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We provide a reduction from the undecidable problem of non-emptiness of the intersection of deterministic one-counter automata (DOCA)
[
Val73
, p. 75]
.
Such an automaton model is similar to pushdown automata, but its stack alphabet is single-letter only.
The
\mathsf{Self}
operator will be especially useful to introduce “disjunction” to paths.

Let
\Sigma
be an alphabet and
\mathtt{w}\coloneqq(\mathtt{a}_{1},\mathtt{b}_{1})\ldots(\mathtt{a}_{n},\mathtt{b}_{n})
be a word over
\Sigma\times\{c,r,i\}
.
We call the word
\pi_{1}(\mathtt{w})\coloneqq\mathtt{a}_{1}\ldots\mathtt{a}_{n}
from
\Sigma^{*}
the
projection
of
\mathtt{w}
.
An important property of DOCAs is that they can be made visibly pushdown in the following sense.

Lemma 4
.

For any deterministic one-counter automaton
\mathdutchbcal{A}
over the alphabet
\Sigma
,
we can construct a visibly-pushdown automaton
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}
over a pushdown alphabet
\tilde{\Sigma}\coloneqq(\Sigma\times\{c\},(\Sigma\times\{i\})\cup\{\mathtt{x}\},\Sigma\times\{r\})
with a fresh internal letter
\mathtt{x}
such that all words in
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}})
are of the form
\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\mathtt{x}\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{2}}\mathtt{x}\ldots\mathtt{x}\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{n}}
for
\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{1}},\ldots,\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{n}}\in\Sigma\times\{c,i,r\}
, and

\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A})=\{\pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathtt{w}})\mid\tilde{\mathtt{w}}\coloneqq\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{n}},\;\;\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\mathtt{x}\ldots\mathtt{x}\tilde{\mathtt{a}_{n}}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}})\}.

Proof 3.1

(Proof sketch.)
.

Alur and Madhusudan proved
[
AM09
, Thm. 5.2]
that for any context-free language
\mathdutchbcal{L}
over
\Sigma
there exists a VPL
\hat{\mathdutchbcal{L}}
, over the pushdown alphabet
(\Sigma\times\{c\},(\Sigma\times\{i\}),\Sigma\times\{r\})
, for which
\mathdutchbcal{L}=\{\pi_{1}(\mathtt{w})\mid\mathtt{w}\in\hat{\mathdutchbcal{L}}\}
holds.
3
3

3

The main proof idea here is to take an input DOCA, and decorate the letters on its transitions with
c
,
i
, and
r
, depending on the counter action of the transition.

Suppose now that a one-counter automaton
\mathdutchbcal{A}
is given.
By means of the previous construction, we obtain a visibly-pushdown automaton
\hat{\mathdutchbcal{A}}\coloneqq(\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{I},\mathtt{F},\Gamma,\mathtt{T})
for which
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A})=\{\pi_{1}(\mathtt{w})\mid\mathtt{w}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\hat{\mathdutchbcal{A}})\}
holds.
What remains to be done is to “insert” the
internal
letter
\mathtt{x}
after every position of a word accepted by
\hat{\mathdutchbcal{A}}
.
As reading internal letters by visibly-pushdown automata do not affect the content of their stacks, we may proceed as in standard constructions from the theory of regular languages
[
Sip13
, Ex. 1.31]
.
As the first step, we expand the set of states
\mathtt{Q}
with fresh states of the form
\mathtt{q}_{\delta}
for all
\delta\in\mathtt{T}
.
As the second step, we “split” every transition
\delta
in
\mathtt{T}
into two “parts”.
Suppose that
\delta
leads from
\mathtt{q}
to
\mathtt{q}^{\prime}
after reading the letter
\mathtt{a}
.
We thus (i) replace
\delta
in
\mathtt{T}
with the transition that transforms
\mathtt{q}
into
\mathtt{q}_{\delta}
after reading
\mathtt{a}
(and has the same effect on the stack as
\delta
has), and (ii) append the transition
(\mathtt{q}_{\delta},\mathtt{x},\mathtt{q}^{\prime})
to
\mathtt{T}
.
Call the resulting automaton
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}
.
It can now be readily verified that
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}})=\{\hat{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\mathtt{x}\hat{\mathtt{a}_{2}}\mathtt{x}\ldots\mathtt{x}\hat{\mathtt{a}_{n}}\mid\hat{\mathtt{w}}\coloneqq\hat{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\ldots\hat{\mathtt{a}_{n}},\ \hat{\mathtt{w}}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\hat{\mathdutchbcal{A}})\}
holds,
and thus, by the relationship between
\mathdutchbcal{A}
and
\hat{\mathdutchbcal{A}}
, the automaton
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}
is as desired.

Let us fix a finite alphabet
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbf{N_{R}}
.
We also fix two deterministic one-counter automata
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}
over
\Sigma
, and let
\mathdutchbcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathdutchbcal{C}_{2}
be deterministic one-counter automata recognizing the complement of the languages of
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}
(they exist as DOCA are closed under complement
[
Val73
, p. 76]
).
Finally, we apply Lemma
4
to construct their visibly-pushdown counterparts
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{1}
,
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{2}
,
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}}_{1}
,
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}}_{2}
over
the same
pushdown alphabet
\tilde{\Sigma}
.
We stress that the letter
\mathtt{x}
, playing the role of a “separator”, is identical for all of the aforementioned visibly-pushdown automata.
Moreover, note that the non-emptiness of
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{1})\cap\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{2})
is not equivalent to the non-emptiness of
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1})\cap\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1})
, as the projection of a letter
\mathtt{a}\in\tilde{\Sigma}
may be used by
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}
in different contexts (
e
.
g
. both as a call or as a return).

We are going to encode words accepted by one-counter automata by means of word-like interpretations.
A pointed interpretation
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is
\Sigma
-friendly
if
for every element
\mathrm{e}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
that is
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-reachable from
\mathrm{d}
in
\mathcal{I}
there exists a unique letter
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
so that
\mathrm{e}
carries all
\tilde{\mathtt{a}}
-self-loops for all
\tilde{\mathtt{a}}\in\tilde{\Sigma}
with
\pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})=\mathtt{a}
, and no self-loops for all other letters in
\tilde{\Sigma}
(also including
\mathtt{x}
).

Observe that
\Sigma
-friendly interpretations can be axiomatised with an
\mathcal{ALC}^{\textsf{Self}}
-concept
\mathrm{C}_{\textrm{fr}}^{\Sigma}
:

\mathrm{C}_{\textrm{fr}}^{\Sigma}\coloneqq\forall{\mathtt{x}^{*}}.\bigsqcup_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\ \mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\mathtt{b}\neq\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma,\pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})=\mathtt{a},\pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathtt{b}})=\mathtt{b}}\ \Big([\exists{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}.\mathsf{Self}]\sqcap\neg[\exists{\tilde{\mathtt{b}}}.\mathsf{Self}]\sqcap\neg[\exists{\mathtt{x}}.\mathsf{Self}]\Big).

Moreover, every
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-path
\rho
in a
\Sigma
-friendly
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})

represents
a word in
\Sigma^{*}
in the following sense: the
i
-th letter of such a word is
\mathtt{a}
if and only if the
i
-th element of the path carries an
(\mathtt{a},c)
-self-loop.
This is well-defined, by the fact that every
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-reachable element in
\Sigma
-friendly
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
carries a
(\mathtt{a},c)
-self-loop for a unique letter
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
. Consult Figure
1
for a visualization.

\mathrm{d}^{\mathcal{I}}
(\mathtt{a},c),(\mathtt{a},r)
(\mathtt{a},i)
(\mathtt{b},c),(\mathtt{b},r)
(\mathtt{b},i)
(\mathtt{b},c),(\mathtt{b},r)
(\mathtt{b},i)
(\mathtt{a},c),(\mathtt{a},r)
(\mathtt{a},i)
(\mathtt{c},c),(\mathtt{c},r)
(\mathtt{c},i)
\mathtt{x}
\mathtt{x}
\mathtt{x}
\mathtt{x}

Figure 1.
An example
\Sigma
-friendly
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
encoding the word
\mathtt{abbac}
.

As a special class of
\Sigma
-friendly interpretations we consider
\Sigma
-metawords.
We say that
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is a
\Sigma
-metaword
if it is a
\Sigma
-friendly interpretation of the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
for some positive
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the role name
\mathtt{x}
is interpreted as the set
\{(i,i{+}1)\mid 0\leq i\leq n{-}2\}
, and all other role names are either interpreted as
\emptyset
or are subsets of the diagonal
\{(i,i)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\}
(or, put differently, they appear only as self-loops).
The example
\Sigma
-friendly
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
from Figure
1
is actually a
\Sigma
-metaword.
Note that for every word
\mathtt{w}\in\Sigma^{+}
there is a
\Sigma
-metaword representing
\mathtt{w}
.
A crucial observation regarding
\Sigma
-metawords is as follows.

{obs}

Let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,
\Sigma
-metaword
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
, and
\tilde{\mathtt{w}}
be in the language of
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
.
Then the element
\mathrm{d}
can
\{\tilde{\mathtt{w}}\}
-reach an element
\mathrm{e}
via a path
\rho
if and only if for all odd indices
i
we have
\rho_{i}=\rho_{i{+}1}
and for all even indices
i
we have
\rho_{i}+1=\rho_{i{+}1}
.

As the next step of the construction, we are going to decorate
\Sigma
-friendly interpretations with extra information on whether or not words represented by paths are accepted by
\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}
.
This is achieved by means of the following concept

\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\coloneqq\mathrm{C}_{\textrm{fr}}^{\Sigma}\sqcap\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{1})}.\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}}_{1})}.\neg\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}},

for a fresh concept name
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}
.
We define the concept
\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}
analogously.
We have that:

Lemma 5
.

Fix
\ell\in\{1,2\}
.
If
\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
is satisfied by a pointed interpretation
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
,
then
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is
\Sigma
-friendly and for every element
\mathrm{e}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
that is
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-reachable from
\mathrm{d}
via a path
\rho
we have
\mathrm{e}\in(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if the
\Sigma
-word represented by
\rho
belongs to
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
.
Moreover, after reinterpreting the concept name
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
, every
\Sigma
-metaword becomes a model of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
.

Proof 3.2
.

The proof relies on Observation
3
.
Suppose that
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is a pointed interpretation and
\mathrm{d}\in(\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We know that
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is
\Sigma
-friendly by the satisfaction of
\mathrm{C}_{\textrm{fr}}^{\Sigma}
.
For the remainder of the proof, consider any element
\mathrm{e}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
that is
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-reachable from
\mathrm{d}
, say, via a path
\rho\coloneqq\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{n}
.
Let
\mathtt{w}
be the word represented by
\rho
.
This implies, that for every index
i
, the element
\rho_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
is equipped with a family of self-loops involving (a decorated) letter
\mathtt{w}_{i}
.
We consider two cases:

•

Assume that
\mathrm{e}\in(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We will show that
\mathtt{w}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
.
Ad absurdum, suppose that
\mathtt{w}\not\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
.
Then, by definition of
\mathdutchbcal{C}_{\ell}
, we have that
\mathtt{w}
belongs to
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{C}_{\ell})
.
By the construction of
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}_{\ell}}
there exists a sequence
\star_{1},\ldots,\star_{n}\in\{c,i,r\}
, for which the word
\mathtt{u}\coloneqq(\mathtt{w}_{1},\star_{1})\mathtt{x}\ldots\mathtt{x}(\mathtt{w}_{n},\star_{n})
is accepted by
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}_{\ell}}
.
But then the path
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq\rho_{1}\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\rho_{2}\ldots\rho_{n}\rho_{n}
witnesses
\{\mathtt{u}\}
-reachability (and thus
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}_{\ell}})
-reachability) of
\mathrm{e}
from
\mathrm{d}
.
Due to the satisfaction of
\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}}_{\ell})}.\neg\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
by
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
we infer
\mathrm{e}\in(\neg\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
A contradiction.

•

Assume that
\mathtt{w}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
.
We proceed analogously to the previous case.
By the construction of
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
there exists a sequence
\star_{1},\ldots,\star_{n}\in\{c,i,r\}
, for which the word
\mathtt{u}\coloneqq(\mathtt{w}_{1},\star_{1})\mathtt{x}\ldots\mathtt{x}(\mathtt{w}_{n},\star_{n})
is accepted by
\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
.
Once again, the path
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq\rho_{1}\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\rho_{2}\ldots\rho_{n}\rho_{n}
witnesses
\{\mathtt{u}\}
-reachability (and thus
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})
-reachability) of
\mathrm{e}
from
\mathrm{d}
.
Due to the satisfaction of
\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{\ell})}.\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
by
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
we infer
\mathrm{e}\in(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}})^{\mathcal{I}}
, as desired.

For the last statement of the proof, take a
\Sigma
-metaword
\mathcal{I}
that represents a word
\mathtt{w}\in\Sigma^{*}
.
We alter the interpretation of the concept name
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
in
\mathcal{I}
so that
(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}^{\mathcal{I}})=\{i{-}1\mid\mathtt{w}_{1}\ldots\mathtt{w}_{i}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})\}
.
It follows that
\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{A}}_{\ell})}.\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}\sqcap\forall{\mathdutchbcal{L}(\tilde{\mathdutchbcal{C}}_{\ell})}.\neg\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
is indeed satisfied by
(\mathcal{I},0)
.

Equipped with Lemma
5
, we are ready to prove correctness of our reduction.

Lemma 6
.

\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}\sqcap\exists{\mathtt{x}^{*}}.\left(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}{\sqcap}\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}\right)
is satisfiable iff
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1})\cap\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof 3.3
.

For one direction, take
\mathtt{w}\in\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1})\cap\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2})
.
Let
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
be a
\Sigma
-metaword representing
\mathtt{w}
.
By Lemma
5
we decorate
\mathcal{I}
with concepts
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}
so that
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})\models\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}
, and the interpretations of concepts
\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell}}
contain precisely the elements
k
for which the
k
-letter prefix of
\mathtt{w}
belongs to
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
.
In particular, this means that
(|\mathtt{w}|{-}1)\in(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
As
(|\mathtt{w}|{-}1)
is
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-reachable from
0
, we conclude the satisfaction of
\exists{\mathtt{x}^{*}}.\left(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}\right)
by
(\mathcal{I},0)
.
Hence, the concept from the statement of Lemma
6
is indeed satisfiable.

For the other direction, assume that
(\mathcal{I},\mathrm{d})
is a model of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{C}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}\sqcap\exists{\mathtt{x}^{*}}.\left(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}}\right)
.
Then there exists an
\mathtt{x}^{*}
-path
\rho
from
\mathrm{d}
to some
\mathrm{e}\in(\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1}}\sqcap\mathrm{Acc}_{\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2}})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Hence, by Lemma
5
, for all
\ell\in\{1,2\}
the word represented by
\rho
belongs to
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{\ell})
, and thus also
\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{1})\cap\mathdutchbcal{L}(\mathdutchbcal{A}_{2})
.

By the undecidability of the non-emptiness problem for intersection of one-counter languages
[
Val73
, p. 75]
, we conclude Theorem
7
.

Theorem 7
.

The concept satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{ALC}^{\textsf{Self}}_{\mathsf{vpl}}
is undecidable, even if only visibly-pushdown languages that are encodings of DOCA languages are allowed in concepts.

There is nothing special about deterministic one-counter automata used in the proof.
In fact, any automaton model would satisfy our needs as long as it would (i) have an undecidable non-emptiness problem for the intersection of languages, (ii) enjoy the analogue of Lemma
4
, and (iii) be closed under complement.
We leave it is an open problem to see if there exists a
single
visibly-pushdown language
\mathdutchbcal{L}
that makes the concept satisfiability of
\mathcal{ALC}^{\textsf{Self}}_{\mathsf{reg}}
extended with
\mathdutchbcal{L}
undecidable.
For instance, the decidability status of
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
with
\mathsf{Self}
is open.

4.
Negative results II: Nominals meet
{\mathit{r}}^{\#}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathit{s}}^{\#}

We next provide an undecidability proof for the concept satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{ALCO}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
.
To achieve this, we employ a slight variant of the classical domino tiling problem
[
Wan61
]
.

A
domino tiling system
is a triple
\mathdutchcal{D}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Col},\mathrm{T},\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

is a finite set of

We also say that tiles
\mathrm{t}\coloneqq(\mathrm{c}_{l},\mathrm{c}_{d},\mathrm{c}_{r},\mathrm{c}_{u})

-compatible
if

-compatible
if
\mathrm{c}_{u}=\mathrm{c}_{d}^{\prime}
.
We say that
\mathdutchcal{D}

covers

\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
(where

are positive integers) if there exists a mapping

such that for all pairs

with

the following conditions are satisfied:

(TBorders):

iff

;

iff

iff
\mathrm{c}_{d}=\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 93.81 -83.35 h 9.22 v 9.22 h -9.22 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.1319pt}{-56.90552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
;

(THori):

If
(x{+}1,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
then
\xi(x,y)
and
\xi(x{+}1,y)
are
\mathrm{H}
-compatible.

(TVerti):

If
(x,y{+}1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
then
\xi(x,y)
and
\xi(x,y{+}1)
are
\mathrm{V}
-compatible.

Intuitively,
\xi:\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
can be seen as a white-bordered rectangle of size
n\times m
coloured by unit
4
-sided tiles (with coordinates corresponding to the left, down, right, and upper colour) from
\mathrm{T}
, where sides of tiles of consecutive squares have matching colours.

{exa}

Suppose that
\mathrm{Col}=\{\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

W.l.o.g. we assume that
\mathrm{T}
does not contain tiles having more than
2
white sides.
A system
\mathdutchcal{D}
is
solvable
if there exist positive integers
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\mathdutchcal{D}
covers
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
.
The problem of deciding whether an input domino tiling system is solvable is undecidable, which can be shown by a minor modification of classical undecidability proofs
[
PH23
, Lemma 3.9]
[
Boa97
]
.
For a domino tiling system
\mathdutchcal{D}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Col},\mathrm{T},\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

we employ fresh concept names from
\mathrm{C}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}\coloneqq\{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{t}}\mid\mathrm{t}\in\mathrm{T}\}
to encode mappings
\xi

and

(where the individual names from

{defi}

Consider a domino tiling system
\mathdutchcal{D}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Col},\mathrm{T},\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 93.81 -83.35 h 9.22 v 9.22 h -9.22 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.1319pt}{-56.90552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
.
An interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-
snake
whenever all seven criteria listed below are fulfilled:

(SPath):

There is an
\mathit{r}^{+}
-path
\rho
that starts in
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then passes through
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then passes through
\texttt{lu}^{\mathcal{I}}
and finishes in
\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
More formally, there are indices
1<i<j<|\rho|
such that
\rho_{1}=\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\rho_{i}=\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\rho_{j}=\texttt{lu}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\rho_{|\rho|}=\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

(SNoLoop):

No
\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
-named element can
\mathit{r}^{+}
-reach itself.

(SUniqTil):

For every element
\mathrm{d}
that is
\mathit{r}^{*}
-reachable from
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
there exists precisely one tile
\mathrm{t}\in\mathrm{T}
such that
\mathrm{d}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\mathcal{I}}
(we say that
\mathrm{d}
is
labelled
by a tile
\mathrm{t}
or that
\mathrm{d}

carries

\mathrm{t}
).

(SSpecTil):

The
\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
-named elements are unique elements
\mathit{r}^{*}
-reachable from
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
that are labelled by tiles with two white sides.
Moreover, we have that
(a)
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
carries a tile that is left-border and down-border,
(b)
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
carries a tile that is right-border and down-border,
(c)
\texttt{lu}^{\mathcal{I}}
carries a tile that is left-border and up-border,
(d)
\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}
carries a tile that is right-border and up-border.

(SHori):

For all elements
\mathrm{d}
different from
\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}
that are
\mathit{r}^{*}
-reachable from
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
and labelled by some tile
\mathrm{t}\coloneqq(\mathrm{c}_{l},\mathrm{c}_{d},\mathrm{c}_{r},\mathrm{c}_{u})
, there exists a tile
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\mathrm{c}_{l}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}_{d}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}_{r}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}_{u}^{\prime})
for which all
\mathit{r}
-successors
\mathrm{e}
of
\mathrm{d}
carry the tile
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}
and: (i)
\mathrm{t},\mathrm{t}^{\prime}
are
\mathrm{H}
-compatible,
(ii) if
\mathrm{c}_{d}=\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.1319pt}{-56.90552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.1319pt}{-56.90552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
.

(SLen):

There exists a unique positive integer
\mathrm{N}
such that all
\mathit{r}^{+}
-paths between
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
are of length
\mathrm{N}{-}1
. Moreover,
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
is the only element
\mathit{r}^{\mathrm{N}{-}1}
-reachable from
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

(SVerti):

For all elements
\mathrm{d}
that are
\mathit{r}^{*}
-reachable from
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
and labelled by some
\mathrm{t}\in\mathrm{T}
that is not up-border, we have that (a) there exists a tile
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}
such that all elements
\mathrm{e}

\mathit{r}^{\mathrm{N}}
-reachable (for
\mathrm{N}
guaranteed by
(SLen):
) from
\mathrm{d}
carry
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}
, (b)
\mathrm{t}
and
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}
are
\mathrm{V}
-compatible, (c)
\mathrm{t}
is left-border (resp. right-border) if and only if
\mathrm{t}^{\prime}
is.

Note that tiles are not “deterministic” in the following sense: it could happen that two elements carry the same tile but tiles of their (horizontal or vertical) successors do not coincide.

If
\mathcal{I}
satisfies all but the last two conditions, we call it a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-
pseudosnake
.
The key properties of our encoding are extracted and established in Lemmas
8
–
9
.

Lemma 8
.

If a domino tiling system
\mathdutchcal{D}
is solvable then there exists a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-snake.

Proof 4.1
.

Suppose that
\mathdutchcal{D}
covers
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and let
\xi
be a mapping witnessing it.
Define an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
as follows:

(1)

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{n\cdot m}
,

(2)

\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq 0
,

\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq n{-}1
,

\texttt{lu}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq(m{-}1)\cdot n
,

\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq m\cdot n-1
,
and
\texttt{a}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq 0
for all
\texttt{a}\in\mathbf{N_{I}}\setminus\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

(3)

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\{(x+y\cdot n)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\mid\xi(x,y)=\mathrm{t}\}
for all
\mathrm{t}\in\mathrm{T}
, and
\mathrm{C}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\emptyset
for
\mathrm{C}\in\mathbf{N_{C}}\setminus\mathrm{C}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
,

(4)

\mathit{r}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\{(i,i{+}1)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n\cdot m-1}\}
, and
(\mathit{r}^{\prime})^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq\emptyset
for all
\mathit{r}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N_{R}}\setminus\{\mathit{r}\}
.

Thus
\mathcal{I}
is an
n\cdot m
element
\mathit{r}^{+}
-path, labelled accordingly to
\xi
.
As
\xi
respects
(TBorders):
,
(THori):
and
(TVerti):
, we can readily verify that
\mathcal{I}
is indeed a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-snake.
The only case that requires treatment, is to verify the satisfaction of
(SHori):
for elements of the form
\mathrm{d}\coloneqq(n{-}1)+y\cdot n
for some
y\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, by
(TBorders):
,
\mathrm{d}
carries a right-border tile and its
\mathit{r}
-successor
\mathrm{d}^{\prime}\coloneqq 0+(y{+}1)\cdot n
carries a left-border tile.
Hence, their tiles are
\mathrm{H}
-compatible.

Lemma 9
.

If there exists a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-snake for a domino tiling system
\mathdutchcal{D}
, then
\mathdutchcal{D}
is solvable.

Proof 4.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}
is a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-snake.
Let
\rho\coloneqq\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{|\rho|}
be the path guaranteed by
(SPath):
and let
\mathrm{N}
be the integer guaranteed by
(SLen):
.
We show by induction that
|\rho|
is divisible by
\mathrm{N}
.
The inductive assumption states that for all integers
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\cdot\mathrm{N}\leq\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert
we have:

(1)

\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+1}
carries a left-border tile,

(2)

There is no
2\leq i<\mathrm{N}
such that
\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+i}
carries a left-border tile or a right-border tile,

(3)

\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}}
carries a right-border tile.

Then by Property (iii) and the fact that
\rho_{|\rho|}
(equal to
\texttt{ru}^{\mathcal{I}}
by
(SPath):
) carries a right-border tile (by Property (d) of
(SSpecTil):
), we can conclude that
|\rho|
is indeed divisible by
\mathrm{N}
.

We heavily rely on the fact that every element of
\rho
is labelled by precisely one tile, which is due to
(SUniqTil):
.
We start with the case of
k=0
.
Then
\rho_{0\cdot\mathrm{N}+1}=\rho_{1}
is equal to
\texttt{ld}^{\mathcal{I}}
, by
(SPath):
.
Moreover,
\rho_{1}
is labelled with a left-border tile, by Property (a) of
(SSpecTil):
.
What is more,
\rho_{0\cdot\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}}=\rho_{\mathrm{N}}
is equal to
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
(by
(SLen):
), which carries a right-border tile by Property (b) of
(SSpecTil):
.
This resolves Properties (i) and (iii).
To establish Property (ii), assume towards a contradiction that there is
i
between
2
and
\mathrm{N}
for which
\rho_{i}
carries a left-border tile (the proof for a right-border tile is analogous).
Take the smallest such
i
.
By
(SHori):
we infer that
\rho_{(i{-}1)}
carries a right-border tile.
In particular, this means that
i>2
because the tile carried by

\rho_{1}
is not right-border.
By exhaustive application of
(SHori):
and the fact that the tile of
\rho_{1}
is down-border, we deduce that the tile of
\rho_{(i{-}1)}
is also down-border.
Hence, by Property (b) of
(SSpecTil):
we have that
\rho_{(i{-}1)}
is equal to
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
But then the path
\rho_{(i{-}1)}\rho_{i}\ldots\rho_{\mathrm{N}}
witnesses
\mathit{r}^{+}
-reachability of
\texttt{rd}^{\mathcal{I}}
from itself, which is forbidden by
(SNoLoop):
. A contradiction.
For the inductive step, assume that Properties (i)–(iii) hold true for some
k
, and consider the case of
k{+}1
.
Note that Property (i) follows from Property (iii) of the inductive assumption by
(SHori):
.
We next show that Property (ii) holds. Assume ad absurdum that there is
i
for which
\rho_{(k{+}1)\cdot\mathrm{N}+i}
carries a left-border (resp. right-border) tile. But then, invoking Item (c) of
(SVerti):
, we infer that
\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+i}
is also left-border (resp. right-border). This contradicts Property (ii) of the inductive assumption. Hence Property (ii) holds true.
By inductive assumption, we know that
\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}}
carries a right-border tile.
Then, we apply Property (c) of
(SVerti):
to infer that
\rho_{k\cdot\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}}=\rho_{(k{+}1)\cdot\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{N}}
is right-border, as desired. This establishes Property (iii), and concludes the induction.

Let
\mathrm{M}\coloneqq|\rho|/\mathrm{N}
.
By the previous claim, we know that
\mathrm{M}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consider a function
\xi:\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{N}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}\to\mathrm{T}
that maps all
(x,y)
to the unique tile carried by
\rho_{x+\mathrm{N}\cdot y+1}
. (Note that we number paths from
1
!)
This function is well-defined by
(SUniqTil):
and it satisfies
(THori):
and
(TVerti):
due to the satisfaction of
(SHori):
and
(SVerti):
.
The satisfaction of the first two statements of
(TBorders):
by
\xi
is guaranteed by Properties (i)–(iii) from the induction above.
Finally, the last two statements of
(TBorders):
are due to straightforward induction that employs
(SSpecTil):
and the last statement of
(SHori):
.
As we proved that
\xi
covers
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{N}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}
, we conclude that
\mathdutchcal{D}
is indeed solvable.

While
\mathdutchcal{D}
-snakes do not seem to be directly axiomatizable even in
\mathcal{ALC}_{\textsf{vpl}}
, we at least see how to express
\mathdutchcal{D}
-pseudosnakes in
\mathcal{ALCO}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
. The next lemma is routine.

Lemma 10
.

For every domino tiling system
\mathdutchcal{D}\coloneqq(\mathrm{Col},\mathrm{T},\hbox to7.07pt{\vbox to7.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-67.59889pt\lower-60.43852pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
,
there exists an
\mathcal{ALCO}_{\textsf{reg}}^{\mathit{r}\#\mathit{s}\#}
-concept
\mathrm{C}_{\includegraphics[scale]{graphics/icons8-snake-30.png}}{D}
, that employs only the role
\mathit{r}
, individual names from
\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
and concept names from
\mathrm{C}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}
, such that for all interpretations
\mathcal{I}
we have that
\mathcal{I}
is a
\mathdutchcal{D}
-pseudosnake iff
\mathcal{I}\models\mathrm{C}_{\includegraphics[scale]{graphics/icons8-snake-30.png}}{D}
.

Proof 4.3
.

We present a rather straightforward axiomatization of the aforementioned properties, written from the point of view of the interpretation of a nominal
ld
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:Path}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\texttt{ld}\}\sqcap\exists{\mathit{r}^{+}}.\left(\{\texttt{rd}\}\sqcap\exists{\mathit{r}^{+}}.\left(\{\texttt{lu}\}\sqcap\exists{\mathit{r}^{+}}.\{\texttt{ru}\}\right)\right).

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:NoLoop}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\texttt{a}\in\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}}\forall{\mathit{r}^{*}}.\Big[\{\texttt{a}\}\to\forall{\mathit{r}^{+}}.\neg\{\texttt{a}\}\Big].

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:UniqTil}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\forall{\mathit{r}^{*}}\big[\bigsqcup_{\mathrm{C}\in\mathrm{C}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}}\left(\mathrm{C}\sqcap\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}},\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\neq\mathrm{C}}\neg\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\right)\Big].

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:SpecTil}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\forall{\mathit{r}^{*}}\big[\left(\bigsqcup_{\mathrm{t}\in\mathrm{T}\ \text{with two white sides}}\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{t}}\right)\leftrightarrow\bigsqcup_{\texttt{a}\in\mathsf{N}_{\text{\faIcon{puzzle-piece}}}^{\mathrm{T}}}\{\texttt{a}\}\big]\sqcap\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:SpecTil}}}^{\text{down}}\sqcap\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:SpecTil}}}^{\text{up}},\ \text{where}

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:SpecTil}}}^{\text{down}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\{\texttt{ld}\}\sqcap\bigsqcup_{\mathrm{t}\coloneqq(\hbox to5.07pt{\vbox to5.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-68.59879pt\lower-59.43863pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.1319pt}{-56.90552pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{\ref{snake:SpecTil}}}^{\text{up}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\exists{\mathit{r}^{*}}.\left(\{\texttt{lu}\}\sqcap\bigsqcup_{\mathrm{t}\coloneqq(\hbox to5.07pt{\vbox to5.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-68.59879pt\lower-59.43863pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
